Use Intelligent Terminal experimentally beside your normal Windows Terminal.

Keep normal Windows Terminal as the stable path.

Use Intelligent Terminal for:

```text

```

Do not use it yet for:

```text
full repo automation
autonomous background edits
release operations
public paper generation
```

Microsoft’s current release is explicitly experimental and installed side-by-side rather than replacing ordinary Windows Terminal. ([Windows Central][1])

That side-by-side design is good for your project.

www.quantamagazine.org
Entanglement Builds Space-Time. Now “Magic” Gives It Gravity. | Quanta Magazine
By Charlie Wood June 3, 2026
14 - 17 minutes

In holographic theories, physicists may have traced the pliability of space-time to its quantum roots: a measure of quantumness known as “magic.”

In 1973, John Archibald Wheeler described the relationship between space and matter in two sentences: “Space acts on matter, telling it how to move. In turn, matter reacts back on space, telling it how to curve.” Wheeler’s words serve as a pithy encapsulation of general relativity, Albert Einstein’s theory of gravity.

Wheeler’s sentences also lay out a challenge that theorists face today: When they build a model of the universe — at least one that works at the quantum level — it’s been difficult to get space and matter to interact in the way that they must.

Einstein cast gravity not as a force but as the geometric bending of space and time. In a popular analogy, the fabric of space-time is like the flat expanse of a mattress, and a massive object like a star is like a bowling ball sitting on top. The weight of the bowling ball compresses the mattress, forming a dimple — matter tells space-time how to curve.

In this analogy, a planet is like a smaller ball. If it rolls close enough to the bowling ball, its path will be altered by the dimple in the mattress — space-time tells matter how to move.

But general relativity has a fatal flaw. When a star dies and collapses, its mass is concentrated into an unimaginably dense point. The dimple in the mattress stretches into a deep depression, one that essentially rips all the way through. Physicists call this arrangement a black hole. If a ball reaches such a rip, it’s no longer guided by the fabric, and the analogy breaks down; scientists need a new theory to understand this and other, similarly extreme situations.

In the late 1990s, physicists had a stroke of luck. They learned that if they imagined space-time as a collection of purely quantum particles, they could in principle describe a black hole — rip and all — in an entirely new way.

Theorists have spent the last few decades trying to understand exactly how a space-time constructed from such quantum particles could work. And they’ve made progress: They’ve found that entanglement between particles gives space-time its structure, building an environment where matter can move — and satisfying the conditions of Wheeler’s first statement. But the origin of Wheeler’s second statement remained mysterious; in their models, matter didn’t tell space how to curve. The bowling ball sat atop the mattress without making a dent.

Until now. Physicists including Charles Cao at Virginia Tech have recently determined how quantum particles could give space-time its bendiness. In a handful of recent works, multiple teams have identified a feature of quantum mechanics that Cao calls “the fabric softener of space.” It’s a measure of quantumness called “magic.”

“Without magic, things are a little too simple,” said John Preskill, a physicist at the California Institute of Technology who contributed to Cao’s newest paper. “And, you know, quantum space-time isn’t quite that simple.”
How To Code a Universe

Perspective shifts abound in physics. For instance, there’s more than one way to look at the motion of a pendulum. You might specify its location using the height and the horizontal displacement of the weight hanging at the end of the string. Or you might use the length of the string and its angle instead. The perspectives are equivalent; simple trigonometric equations take you from one perspective to the other.

Mark Belan/Quanta Magazine

For 50 years, theorists have been chasing a far more profound perspective shift: a new way, beyond Einstein’s curved space-time, to look at the universe.

In the early 1970s, Jacob Bekenstein and Stephen Hawking took the first step in that direction when they discovered that you could reinterpret a black hole (and anything that had fallen into it) as a spherical collection of particles. In the late 1990s, Juan Maldacena, Edward Witten, and others extended this insight to a whole universe; they described an exotic, static world as a throng of interacting particles, also arranged in a sphere.

In both cases, you could replace the 3D region of space-time with particles on the region’s surface. You could consider the surface to be 2D, like a globe flattened into a paper map. Physicists call this dual nature of space-time the holographic principle, since it resembles the way a holographic sticker can cram a whole 3D scene onto a flat surface without losing data.

Over the last couple of decades, theorists have explored what gives the 3D fabric of space its shape. Entanglement, a quantum property that links particles to one another, seems to serve as space’s connective tissue. Take, for instance, a wormhole, a theoretical bridge connecting two distant regions of space. Holographically, a 3D wormhole is equivalent to two entangled sets of particles. Start snipping the “threads” of entanglement that link one set with the other, and the tunnel connecting the regions gets thinner and thinner. Cut the final thread, and the connection dissolves entirely.

Cao learned about the link between entanglement and space as a graduate student at Caltech in 2016, most notably through a paper by Daniel Harlow, a physicist now at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology. “Charles spent a month understanding the paper,” said Jason Pollack, then a fellow graduate student, now a physicist at Syracuse University.

Harlow, building in part on the work of Preskill and others, had identified the type of math required to shift perspectives from 2D to 3D. He needed to encode a space and its matter — stars and planets and electrons — into a bunch of quantum particles. So why not use a quantum error-correcting code?

Quantum error-correcting codes are crucial to quantum computing because quantum computers work by manipulating “qubits,” quantum versions of bits that can exist in superpositions of 0s and 1s. Qubits are extremely delicate, frequently losing their superposition and therefore their extra information. And so physicists have worked out ways to protect this delicate information through redundancy. By spreading out one qubit’s information among many qubits, they can preserve it even if some of the qubits are lost.

The same type of redundancy shows up in holography. “When you design codes for quantum computing, you’re doing the same kind of thing that [holography] already did for you,” said Bartek Czech, a physicist at Tsinghua University in China. A single holographic location — a region of space and the matter in it — is not encoded in just one set of quantum particles; rather, it is spread across many sets, due to their entanglement. Harlow and collaborators detailed how this works in a code in 2014, and he further fleshed out the relationship in the 2016 paper that impressed Cao.

But these codes, known as “stabilizer codes,” had a shortcoming. They divided the entanglement of the particles into two types: one responsible for space and another responsible for matter. And the divide was unbridgeable. Such a perfect split is a virtue in quantum computing, since you want your encrypted data to stay perfectly isolated from the corrupting influence of the outside world. But in holography, that perfection left no room for the two to interact. “We knew how to build a space-time,” Czech said, but “this space-time was inert. It didn’t do anything.”

To get space and matter to interact, Cao knew he needed a more sophisticated code. “It was clear that something else beyond entanglement had to be there,” said Ning Bao, a physicist at Northeastern University.
The Magic Ingredient

Cao started by playing around with existing error-correcting codes. In 2020, he and a collaborator, Brad Lackey, tweaked one such code and found that it allowed space to change — just not in response to matter. It wasn’t gravity, but it was progress. Except that Cao and Lackey didn’t fully understand why the tweak worked.

Circular image with repeating tiles of fish

M.C. Escher’s 1959 woodcut Circle Limit III has the geometry of a holographic world: A whole universe fits inside a spherical surface. In holography, you can learn about what’s happening in the interior by studying the surface itself.

M.C. Escher

The next year, Pollack and his collaborators realized that if you actually tried to create a quantum program that executed the tweaked code on a quantum computer, you’d need to use a particular operation known as a T gate, which rotates a qubit.

Cao took notice. He had just attended a quantum computing conference where researchers were buzzing about gates like these, in part because they are the key to making quantum computers more powerful than classical computers.

Researchers had previously thought the key was entanglement. They had worked out a way of running software on a classical computer that would mimic a quantum task. When that quantum task involved entangling qubits, quantum computers had an advantage over classical computers, as the classical program took ages to run. But then physicists discovered a way of speeding things up; it turned out that certain classical algorithms could mimic certain entangling operations even on a laptop.

In 2004, Alexei Kitaev and Sergey Bravyi, both then at Caltech, brought researchers’ attention to quantum operations known as non-Clifford gates, which include the T gate. When a quantum program uses these operations, the equivalent classical program takes much, much longer to run. Kitaev and Bravyi described the complexity that these operations introduce as “magic.” The more non-Clifford gates you need to produce a quantum state, the more magical that state is.

After Cao learned about magic and non-Clifford gates, he joined forces with Brian Swingle and Christopher White, both researchers at the University of Maryland. In 2020, they studied collections of particles equivalent to an exotic universe called an anti-de Sitter space. The group found that the particles were highly magical. What would the role of this magic be, they wondered, for the anti-de Sitter space the particles represented?

Cao — in partnership with Alioscia Hamma and others and building on work from Xi Dong, now at the University of California, Santa Barbara — found the answer a few years later. They showed that magic gave space its springiness. Magic, in other words, is connected to space’s ability to bend. And therefore magic is connected to gravity. “If you have one,” Bao said, “you always have the other.”

By early 2026, Cao and his collaborators had all the pieces. They knew that magic made space bend. And they knew that quantum codes got their magic from non-Clifford gates. So Cao, Preskill, and others created a next-generation code to succeed the stabilizer codes Harlow and others had focused on a decade before, when they split encoded space from encoded matter. This new code used lots of non-Clifford gates. The gates made the code magical, letting the entanglement for space and the entanglement for matter affect each other.

“This is pretty cool, because in quantum gravity, we don’t expect the background is fixed,” said Cynthia Keeler, a physicist at Arizona State University who was not involved in the work. “It should fluctuate.”

The essential nature of magic especially intrigues physicists like Swingle, who hope to use it on a quantum computer to simulate how gravity behaves in situations where general relativity fails. “If we need high magic, then we intrinsically need a quantum computer,” Swingle said, “because there’s no other way, in general, to get at that kind of question.”
Gravity From Quantumness

In principle, entanglement and magic could be enough for future physicists to simulate space on a quantum computer. But Cao’s new code still needs a lot of work.

During a talk about it at the American Physical Society’s annual summit in Denver, Cao joked that he was the only speaker who wasn’t actually studying quantum gravity. That’s because his code is still extremely general. It doesn’t describe the kind of space in which we live, doesn’t capture the particular reactions Einstein described, and doesn’t include the ticking of time.

The code is more of a proof of concept of the general shape that a theory of quantum gravity should take. If you want your space to bend, use a magical code. “This gets you a precursor of gravity,” Cao said. “You satisfy one of the necessary conditions. Right now, we are at step 0.5 of 5.”

But even at this early stage, the research program highlights some surprising features that any theory of quantum gravity should have.

Einstein and Wheeler thought of space-time as a large, featureless fabric existing with fixed bends and folds — a typical classical object. But now physicists are learning that the two defining features of quantum mechanics, entanglement and magic, correspond to the two defining features of space, its shape and its flexibility. This suggests that space itself is one of the most quantum things imaginable. “All the familiar aspects of gravity are actually a very direct manifestation of something quantum,” Swingle said.

It also suggests that gravity results from imperfect quantum encoding. Non-magical codes produce inert, gravity-free spaces because they protect their encoded information perfectly. Cao and collaborators have shown that gravity comes from the mixing of the encoded information. So by necessity, the encoding must be approximate, and therefore some aspects of what’s going on in the space-time can’t be perfectly recovered by measuring a subset of the quantum particles in the usual way. This approximation, which would indicate a poorly written code for a quantum computer, is “the reason Newton’s apple fell on him,” Czech said.

Cao, for his part, finds the feature appealing. Quantum error correction and quantum computing are human pursuits, he said. He sees no reason that gravity should accommodate our prejudice for perfection.

Correction: June 3, 2026
Charles Cao was a graduate student at Caltech, and he later held a postdoctoral position at the University of Maryland.

The article previously called the gates that introduce magic Toffoli gates and conflated them with a simpler gate called the T gate. Toffoli gates and T gates are both a part of a wider category called non-Clifford gates, all of which introduce magic.


## Direct answer

For your ToE, this is **one of the most relevant outside ideas you have brought up recently**.

Its significance is:

> **It suggests that spacetime geometry may come from quantum information, and gravitational backreaction may require a deeper kind of quantumness called “magic.”**

In simpler words:

> **Entanglement may help build space, but magic may help space bend.**

That matters because your ToE is centrally about:

```text
pillars
→ seams
→ bridges
→ meaning transport
→ master-action dependency
```

This article is basically about one of the deepest seams possible:

```text
quantum information
→ spacetime geometry
→ gravity/backreaction
```

It does **not** prove your ToE. It does **not** prove our universe is literally built from quantum error-correcting codes. But it is a powerful benchmark for your project because it gives a concrete mathematical picture of how “geometry from quantum structure” might work.

---

# 1. Elementary explanation

Imagine spacetime is like a woven blanket.

Earlier holography work suggested:

> **Entanglement is like the thread that connects the blanket together.**

But a blanket can be connected and still be stiff.

This newer idea says:

> **Magic may be what lets the blanket bend and respond when matter is added.**

So:

```text
entanglement = connection / shape
magic = flexibility / backreaction
```

Backreaction means:

> Matter does not just move through spacetime. Matter changes spacetime.

That is Wheeler’s famous idea:

```text
space tells matter how to move
matter tells space how to curve
```

This article says the first part may come from entanglement, while the second part may require quantum magic.

---

# 2. What “magic” means here

In quantum computing, “magic” does **not** mean fantasy.

It means a kind of quantum resource that makes a quantum state harder to simulate with ordinary classical computers.

Roughly:

```text
Clifford/stabilizer quantum states = easier, less magical
non-Clifford states = harder, more magical
```

Non-Clifford gates, such as T gates, are needed for universal quantum computation. They create states that cannot be efficiently reduced to simpler stabilizer behavior.

In the holography papers behind the article, the important claim is that simple stabilizer-code models can make space-like structure, but they are too rigid to produce nontrivial gravitational backreaction. Cao’s 2023 paper argues that nontrivial area operators require non-local magic, and the 2024 “Gravitational back-reaction is magical” paper connects non-local magic with gravitational backreaction in holographic systems. ([arXiv][1])

Plain version:

> **Simple quantum structure can make an inert spacetime model. More complex quantum structure may be needed for gravity-like bending.**

---

# 3. Why this matters for your ToE

Your project has been asking:

```text
What does it take for one physical language to become another?
```

This article asks:

```text
What quantum structure is needed for spacetime and gravity to emerge?
```

That is deeply aligned.

Your ToE already treats seams as central. This article says the seam between quantum information and gravity may require more than entanglement.

That gives you a sharper conceptual lesson:

```text
quantum correlation alone may be insufficient;
the type and complexity of quantum correlation matters.
```

This matters for `QFT-GR`, `GR-QM`, `STAT`, `COSMO`, the master action, and operational position.

---

# 4. The key mathematical pattern

A simplified holographic formula is the Ryu-Takayanagi / quantum extremal surface idea:

```text
entropy of boundary region
≈ area of bulk surface / 4G
+ bulk matter entropy
```

Plain meaning:

> Information on the boundary tells you about geometry inside the spacetime.

Earlier toy models using exact quantum error-correcting codes could encode bulk information into boundary degrees of freedom, but they tended to produce fixed or inert geometries. The 2026 Cao–Cheng–Karthikeyan–Li–Preskill paper says exact erasure-correcting codes give state-independent area terms and cannot capture gravitational backreaction; incorporating backreaction requires approximate quantum error correction, with tripartite non-local magic controlling the leading matter-geometry coupling in the code’s Choi state. ([arXiv][2])

In plain words:

> **Perfectly protected information gives you a rigid spacetime. Imperfect, magic-enriched encoding gives you a spacetime that can respond.**

That is a very ToE-relevant insight.

---

# 5. Major implication: exactness may not be the whole story

Your repo often tries to make things exact:

```text
residual = 0
proof checked
route closed
```

That discipline is good.

But this article gives a subtle warning:

> **A perfectly exact encoding may be too rigid to model gravity.**

In the holographic-code context, exact quantum error correction separates matter and geometry too cleanly. Gravity needs the two to influence each other. The new work suggests that approximate codes with magic allow that coupling. ([arXiv][2])

For your ToE, that suggests a future distinction:

```text
exact mathematical closure
```

versus:

```text
physically correct flexible coupling
```

That does **not** mean “be sloppy.” It means:

> The right math for gravity may involve controlled approximation, not perfect isolation.

That is a very important lesson for QFT-GR.

* that operational position is proven.

The authors explicitly frame it as addressing state-dependent geometry and backreaction in approximate quantum codes, not as a completed quantum gravity theory. ([arXiv][2])

So the correct classification is:

```text
recoverability
approximate error correction
operational position
```

That third layer may become important if your theory continues toward geometry-from-information.

# Bottom line

The significance for your ToE is:

> **This research suggests that spacetime may require at least two quantum resources: entanglement for structure and magic for gravitational flexibility.**

In simplest words:

> **Entanglement may make space. Magic may let space react.**

For your project, the key implication is:

> **A mature ToE may need to explain not only equations and fields, but also the information structure that makes geometry, position, and backreaction possible.**

What are the implications and significance of this for the ToE?


phys.org
Cutting a photon in two creates an infinite swarm of particles
Sam Jarman
7 - 8 minutes
Photon Shutter

Credit: Image generated by the editorial team using AI for illustrative purposes.

By definition, elementary particles can't be broken into smaller pieces. But in a new theoretical study published in Physical Review Letters, Johannes Skaar and colleagues have revealed what would happen if you tried anyway for a single photon. The answer is deeply strange: attempting to cut a photon in two wouldn't produce two smaller photons, but instead conjure an infinite number of them out of thin air.
Impossible to cut in half

Like any quantum particle, a photon exists simultaneously as a single, localized particle, and an extended wave, spread out across space. For their investigation, Skaar's team considered what would happen if a single photon passed through an optical shutter—essentially a very fast mirror that can be switched on and off to block part of a pulse of light. If the shutter was fast enough, it could intercept the photon mid-pulse, snipping off part of this extended wave.

⯈ A simple follow helps independent science journalism stay visible. Follow Phys.org on Google.

To find out what would happen afterward, the researchers applied quantum equations that describe how the photon's underlying electromagnetic field behaves at the quantum level. Specifically, their analysis tracked precisely how the photon's quantum state would be transformed by the shutter's intervention.
Infinite superposition

Rather than producing a photon on one side and a vacuum on the other, the shutter generates something far more strange and complex: a superposition of states containing infinitely many photons simultaneously.

This happens because, in quantum mechanics, empty space isn't truly empty—in reality, it seethes with fluctuations in the electromagnetic field. By rapidly switching the shutter, the team found that these fluctuations are disturbed—and in doing so, they spontaneously create new photons. Crucially though, if you were to look only at the region immediately either side of where the shutter operated, the state would appear deceptively normal: indistinguishable from a single photon on one side, and a simple vacuum on the other.
Deeper quantum investigations

The result offers a striking illustration of how quantum particles behave differently from everyday objects, and raises deeper questions about how quantum systems are measured and how information is localized in space. In their future research, Skaar and his colleagues now plan to push further—exploring whether the same bizarre physics would apply when more than one photon is involved, or when the analysis is extended to other elementary particles, such as electrons.

Written for you by our author Sam Jarman, edited by Gaby Clark, and fact-checked and reviewed by Robert Egan—this article is the result of careful human work. We rely on readers like you to keep independent science journalism alive. If this reporting matters to you, please consider a donation (especially monthly). You'll get an ad-free account as a thank-you.

## Direct answer

For your ToE, this is **highly relevant as a QFT/locality/operational-position benchmark**, not as proof of your theory.

The key significance is:

> **A “single photon” is not a tiny bead that can simply be cut in half. It is a quantum field state, and trying to sharply localize or truncate it can force the field into a much more complicated state.**

The primary paper is **“A truncated photon”** by Isak Cecil Onsager Rukan, Jan Gulla, and Johannes Skaar. It says that truncating a photon with an optical shutter produces neither a smaller photon nor a simple photon/vacuum split, but a superposition and mixture of photon numbers up to infinity. It also says that locally, away from the transition region, the state can still look like a single photon on one side and vacuum on the other. ([arXiv][1])

For your ToE, the clean lesson is:

```text
local appearance ≠ full global quantum-field state
```

That is extremely important.

---

# 1. Elementary explanation

A photon is often described as a particle of light.

But in quantum field theory, a photon is not like a marble flying through space.

It is more like a special wave-pattern in the electromagnetic field.

The paper asks:

> What happens if you try to cut one photon’s wave-packet with a very fast shutter?

The answer is not:

```text
half photon + half photon
```

It is not even:

```text
one photon on one side + nothing on the other
```

Instead, the full quantum field becomes a complicated state involving possible photon numbers:

```text
0 photons
1 photon
2 photons
3 photons
...
up to infinitely many photon-number components
```

That does **not** mean a detector necessarily sees infinite energy or literally counts infinite photons. It means the mathematical quantum state contains components across infinitely many photon-number sectors.

Plain version:

> **Trying to sharply cut a photon does not divide a particle. It disturbs the field.**

---

# 2. Why this matters for your ToE

Your ToE keeps returning to the same idea:

```text
object
→ field
→ boundary/interface
→ measurement
→ meaning
```

This paper strongly supports that way of thinking.

A photon is not simply an isolated object. Its meaning depends on:

* the electromagnetic field,
* the full wave-packet,
* the boundary/shutter interaction,
* locality,
* vacuum structure,
* measurement region.

That is directly relevant to your project’s emphasis on **seams** and **semantic bridges**.

---

# 3. Biggest conceptual lesson

The biggest lesson is:

> **A local observer can see something simple while the global state is complicated.**

The paper says the truncated state can be locally equivalent to a single photon or vacuum on either side of a narrow transition region, even though the full state is a superposition/mix of photon numbers up to infinity. ([arXiv][1])

That maps directly to your ToE:

```text
local residual looks clean
but global semantic closure may still be open
```

This is one of your project’s deepest recurring lessons.

---

# 4. Pillar implications

## QM pillar

This is directly relevant to quantum mechanics because it challenges simple particle intuition.

It reinforces:

> **A quantum object is not always a little thing with a clean boundary.**

For your QM full target, this supports the need to handle:

```text
superposition
measurement
localization
field states
probability
state preparation
```

Not just Schrödinger evolution.

---

## EM pillar

This is about photons, so it is also an electromagnetic-field issue.

The photon is an excitation of the electromagnetic field.

For EM:

> **The electromagnetic field is not merely a classical wave. Its quantum state structure matters.**

This strengthens the EM-QFT bridge.

---

## QFT pillar

This is probably the most direct pillar.

The result says:

> **Photon number is not preserved by abrupt boundary/time-dependent operations.**

A shutter is a time-dependent interaction with the field. In QFT, time-dependent boundaries or rapid changes can create particles.

So for your QFT pillar:

```text
field state + boundary operation → new particle-number structure
```

This is very relevant.

It also warns against treating “particle count” as always primitive.

---

## STAT pillar

The final state is not a single clean outcome; it is a distribution/superposition over many photon-number possibilities.

That touches STAT because you need:

```text
probability over photon numbers
measurement outcomes
local-vs-global information
```

So this is also relevant to QM-STAT.

---

## Operational position

This is very relevant.

Your operational-position idea is:

```text
Position := timing-window + correlation-consistency constraint satisfiability
```

This photon result supports the spirit of that idea.

Why?

Because “where the photon is” is not simply a primitive fact.

If you try to force a sharp boundary — “photon here, vacuum there” — the full quantum field responds in a complicated way.

So the operational-position lesson is:

> **Localization may be something you infer from field correlations and measurement constraints, not a simple built-in property of the particle.**

In simpler words:

> A photon’s “where” is not just a dot. It is a field-state and measurement-setup question.

---

# 5. Seam implications

## EM-QFT seam

This is an excellent EM-QFT benchmark.

The process is:

```text
single-photon electromagnetic state
→ optical shutter/boundary operation
→ quantum field transformation
→ multiphoton superposition
```

That is exactly a quantum-electromagnetic field bridge.

A mature EM-QFT seam should be able to explain why:

```text
classical-looking optical operation
```

can produce:

```text
nonclassical photon-number structure
```

---

## QM-STAT seam

This paper also matters for measurement and probability.

If the state contains many possible photon-number components, then measurement depends on:

```text
which region you measure
what detector sees
which observable you ask about
```

So it supports your idea that statistical meaning is not automatically given by the formal state alone. You need a measurement/observable bridge.

---

## QFT-GR seam

Not directly.

There is no gravity here.

But the lesson is relevant:

> In field theory, local-looking objects can hide global complexity.

That same lesson may matter in QFT-GR, where local stress-energy and global spacetime consistency may not match simply.

---

# 6. What this says about “particles”

The article’s headline says “cutting a photon in two creates an infinite swarm of particles.”

A more careful wording is:

> **A sharp truncation operation transforms the field state into a complicated state with photon-number components extending to arbitrarily high photon number.**

That is much safer.

For your ToE, the lesson is:

> **Particles may be appearances of field states under specific measurement conditions.**

This supports one of your broader intuitions:

```text
observed object = stable excitation + measurement/interaction context
```

Not:

```text
observed object = tiny hard bead
```

---

# 7. Important math idea

A normal single-photon state can be symbolically written as:

```text
|1⟩
```

A vacuum state is:

```text
|0⟩
```

The naive expectation would be:

```text
truncated photon = |1⟩ on one side + |0⟩ on the other
```

But the paper says the real state is more like:

```text
|ψ⟩ = c0|0⟩ + c1|1⟩ + c2|2⟩ + c3|3⟩ + ...
```

Plain meaning:

> The state includes possible contributions from many photon numbers.

The “infinite” part is this infinite list of possible photon-number sectors.

Again, this is a statement about the quantum state, not a claim that usable infinite energy appears.

---

# 8. Why this matters for residuals

A simple local residual might say:

```text
left side looks like photon
right side looks like vacuum
```

So locally:

```text
residual = 0
```

But globally, the full field state is not simply:

```text
photon + vacuum
```

So your project’s core warning applies:

```text
local residual zero ≠ full global closure
```

This paper is a beautiful physics example of that.

---

# 9. What this suggests about nature

Careful speculation:

## 1. Localization may be expensive

Trying to localize or truncate a quantum field too sharply may generate extra field excitations.

## 2. Vacuum is active

The vacuum is not just empty nothing. It is the baseline quantum field state, and operations on the field can disturb it.

## 3. Particles are not fundamental in the everyday sense

Particle number may depend on the state, region, observer, and operation.

## 4. Boundaries matter

A shutter or boundary is not just a passive wall. It changes the field state.

## 5. Operational position may need field-state support

Position may require stable measurement and correlation structure, not just coordinate assignment.

---

# 10. What this does not prove

This does **not** prove:

* your ToE,
* operational position,
* QFT-GR closure,
* quantum gravity,
* master-action validity,
* that photons can literally be cut into pieces,
* that infinite usable photons or energy appear.

It is a theoretical quantum-optics/QFT result accepted for publication in *Physical Review Letters*. The arXiv record says it was accepted for PRL and gives the DOI-linked record. ([arXiv][1])

---

# 11. Recommended ToE classification

I would classify this as:

```text
EXTERNAL_BENCHMARK_PHOTON_TRUNCATION_LOCALITY_QFT_v0
```

Relevant pillars:

```text
QM
EM
QFT
STAT
Operational Position
```

Relevant seams:

```text
EM-QFT
QM-STAT
field-locality bridge
measurement/localization bridge
```

Status:

```text
NONCLAIM
EXTERNAL_THEORETICAL_BENCHMARK
NOT_TOE_VALIDATION
```

Core benchmark question:

> Can the ToE framework distinguish local photon/vacuum equivalence from the full global quantum-field state?

That is a strong benchmark.

---

# 12. Bottom line

The significance for your ToE is:

> **This paper shows that local particle language can be misleading. A photon is a quantum field state, and forcing a sharp local cut creates global field complexity.**

In simplest words:

> **You cannot understand the photon by treating it like a tiny object. You have to understand the field, the boundary, the measurement, and the full state.**

For your ToE, that supports a major theme:

```text
physical meaning is not just local appearance;
it depends on the full bridge between field structure, measurement, and interpretation.
```

bigthink.com
Gravitational wave astronomy is now a fully mature science
Ethan Siegel
18 - 23 minutes

Here in 2026, something remarkable has happened: the LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA collaboration has just released their results from the second part of their fourth observing run. All told, 161 new gravitational wave events were added to the catalog of successful direct detections, bringing the total number of events up to a staggering 390. Here in 2026, there are:

    four active gravitational wave detectors (with a fifth in the works),
    with sensors, vacuum systems, and quantum-squeezed light that have been upgraded several times,
    now capable of detecting the mergers of compact objects from over 20 billion light-years away,
    spanning mass ranges, for the post-merger remnant, from under 3 solar masses to over 200 solar masses.

It’s a remarkable feat for a field of science that, as little as 11 years ago, had not yet been born. After all, from 1915, when general relativity was first put forth in its final (correct) form until 2015, all attempts at gravitational wave direct detection had only returned null results. The first successful direct detection occurred just days after Advanced LIGO’s original two detectors, in Livingston, LA and Hanford, WA, had started collecting data: on September 14, 2015. A few months later, in early 2016, they announced the results from that detection, where a 36 solar mass and a 29 solar mass black hole had merged into a 62 solar mass object, with the other 3 solar masses worth of energy being carried away in the form of gravitational waves. A tiny amount of energy was deposited in each of LIGO’s two detectors, beginning the era of gravitational wave astronomy.

Here in mid-2026, the number of detection events has skyrocketed to 390, revealing so much about the previously-invisible Universe. Here’s how this all new field of astrophysics has matured so quickly, and what we know today that we didn’t know just a decade ago.
Two bright stars orbiting each other with arrows indicating their paths, surrounded by space. A graph shows orbital phase shift data over time from 1974 to 1982, potentially hinting at gravitational waves, positioned at the bottom right.

As two neutron stars orbit one another, the motion of one mass through the curved spacetime generated by the other mass results in the emission of gravitational waves, which carry energy away and cause the orbits to decay. The first binary neutron star system, where at least one neutron star is a pulsar, was discovered in 1974. Even as early as 1982, as the inset diagram shows, the orbit could be observed decaying, in agreement with general relativity’s predictions.
Credit: NASA, ESA, and A. Feild (STScI) (main); J.H. Taylor & J.M. Weisberg, ApJ, 1982 (inset)

The story of gravitational wave astronomy has progressed rapidly: more rapidly than perhaps anyone could have anticipated. As recently as mid-2015, a full century after Einstein’s general relativity had been put forth, we had only indirect evidence for the existence of gravitational waves: largely inferred by examining the decaying orbits of binary, pulsing neutron stars. If the orbits are decaying, then they’re losing energy, and since energy is conserved for these systems, something must be carrying that energy away. Einstein’s general relativity predicted that the culprit ought to be gravitational waves, but until we observed them directly, we couldn’t be sure.

Gravitational waves are predicted to be quadrupolar in nature: when they pass through a region of space, they alternately compress/shrink and rarify/expand space in mutually perpendicular directions. By building two very long interferometer arms at right angles to one another and sending light down-and-back, very tiny changes in the path length of the two arms relative to one another could be detected by observing changes in how the light from both directions interferes when brought back together. As a gravitational wave passes through, those changes would be detectable by a sensitive enough detector. That design scheme is at the heart of all successful ground-based gravitational wave detectors.

When the two arms of an optical interferometer are of exactly equal length and there is no gravitational wave passing through, the signal is null and the interference pattern is constant. As the arm lengths change, the signal is real and oscillatory, and the interference pattern changes with time in a predictable fashion. This technique is what is used to directly reveal the presence of gravitational waves.
Credit: NASA’s The Space Place

Beginning in the late 20th century, scientists seeking to detect these waves designed and built a prototype detector: the original LIGO. It wasn’t intended to see gravitational waves — not unless something unexpected happened, like a black hole-black hole merger in our own galaxy — but rather to demonstrate the feasibility of this technological approach for seeing gravitational waves. The success of LIGO in meeting their instrumentation and noise goals in the 2000s paved the way for Advanced LIGO: with the twin detectors in Hanford and Livingston beginning science operations in 2015.

Three magazine covers: two marked "SOLD OUT" and one labeled "COMING SOON." Text below announces a new issue releasing this summer for Big Think members.

When the first signal arrived, many assumed it was either a test being conducted, a mistake, or a prank. After all, based on the detection sensitivities and theoretical predictions, only a handful of events were expected, at most, over several months of operations. Who could have imagined that a bona fide signal would arrive just days after the machine was turned on?

Yet that’s precisely what happened, and just a few months later, the detection was announced to the world. With a signal appearing simultaneously in both detectors, LIGO’s executive director David Reitze began February 16, 2016’s address by simply stating, “Ladies and gentlemen, we have detected gravitational waves. We did it.”

GW150914 was the first ever direct detection and proof of the existence of gravitational waves. The waveform, detected by both LIGO observatories, Hanford and Livingston, matched the predictions of general relativity for a gravitational wave emanating from the inward spiral and merger of a pair of black holes of around 36 and 29 solar masses and the subsequent “ringdown” of the single resulting black hole.
Credit: Aurore Simonnet/LIGO Scientific Collaboration

Back in 1609, Galileo turned a modified spyglass skyward for the first time: heralding the era of the telescope. In 1987, neutrinos from a supernova in a nearby galaxy arrived in a particle physics detector, beginning the age of particle astrophysics for objects other than the Sun. (Solar neutrinos had been previously detected beginning in 1968.) But beginning in 2015, we could now see the Universe in an entirely new way: through the gravitational wave signals, or ripples in spacetime, that emerge from massive, compact objects accelerated in strong gravitational fields. A whole new type of astronomy had just been born.

By the end of LIGO’s first observing run in 2016, we had seen two events, later upgraded to three when LVT151012 was reanalyzed in 2018. At the end of the second observing run, after being joined by the Virgo detector in Europe, the total number of detections had increased to 11 by the close of 2018. By the end of 2020, the third observing run — ended earlier than planned due to complications from the COVID-19 pandemic — had brought us up to a total of 90 detections, with Japan’s KAGRA detector joining the LIGO-Virgo collaboration in science operations in early 2020. And the fourth observing run, broken up into three parts spanning from May 2023 to November of 2025, has now released the results from its first two portions (O4A and O4B), bringing our total number of detections up to 390.

We went from no detections to nearly 400 detections (with that number expected to pass 500 with the results from the third portion of the fourth run, O4C) in just the span of a single decade.
source of squeezed light LIGO

This view shows the source of squeezed light within LIGO’s vacuum chamber, taken when the chamber holding the technology was open for maintenance between 2020 and 2023. The frequency-dependent squeezing cavity is not in operation at the time of this photograph, but will be filled with green laser light in a room evacuated of air (and hence, inaccessible to humans) during science operations.
Credit: Wenxuan Jia/ Massachusetts Institute of Technology

One of main reasons for this rapid increase is the fact that, during the time in between various data runs, upgrades to the systems are conducted. That includes improvements to:

    the vacuum system that the light travels through,
    the reflectivity of the mirrors that reflect the light back-and-forth in the interferometer arms,
    the suspension systems that maintain the stability of the detection apparatus,
    the power of the lasers that generate the light critical to the observatories,
    quieting the noise that still remains in the detector’s readouts,
    and the implementation of all-new quantum squeezing techniques to surpass the previous quantum limits of precision.

With each set of improvements, the capabilities of these gravitational wave observatories expand. Signals become clearer and more prominent, and the detection significance of each event increases. The stronger and cleaner the signal in a larger number of detectors, the better we can perform sky localization: determining where in the Universe the merger event occurred. The distance range over which the detectors are sensitive to increases, and the mass range, particularly at the edges of the detection limits, increases as well.

One of the most powerful illustrations to come out of the latest release from the LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA collaboration is shown below, which breaks down the results of the four main observing runs by color, distance (farther from the center), and time (clockwise, starting at the 12 o’clock position) from 2015 to the present.
Two circular plots illustrate detected gravitational wave events, showcasing how gravitational wave astronomy has become a mature science; events are color-coded by observation run and sized by mass, distance, time, and signal-to-noise ratio, with a legend for clarity.

This “petrie dish” plot of gravitational wave detections shows the results from four different runs: O1 in blue, O2 in orange, O3 in green, and O4 split up into O4A (in red) and O4B (in violet), with O4C still to come. Time goes clockwise from 2015 at the 12 o’ clock position to 2026 at present, distance to the event is coded by distance from the center, shading represents the significance of the detection, and circle size represents the mass of the merger event. All 390 detections are shown here.
Credit: D. Davis, R. Udall/LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA Collaboration

As you can see, the number of detections has increased significantly with each run, as have the significances of the detections and the distances out to which detections can occur. Initially, all of the events were located within just a few billion light-years of Earth, which is important in an observable Universe 46 billion light-years in extent. Remember how volume works in our three-dimensional Universe: if you can upgrade your detector so that you’re sensitive to distances that are twice as far as they were previously, you probe eight times the initial volume, which should octuple your expected event rate. The fact that we only saw two (later, upgraded to three) events in O1 and then eight in O2 was actually great news: leading to hundreds of events at present.

In many ways, the results from gravitational wave astronomy provide our best windows into the Universe as far as understanding the population of stellar mass (as opposed to supermassive) black holes. Back in 2015, there were more than a dozen known stellar mass black holes from electromagnetic observations: because we saw either flares or signals arising from a companion orbiting a black hole within our galaxy. While the number of electromagnetic black holes has subsequently increased — to approximately 50 — the number of black holes observed from gravitational waves is well into the hundreds, even approaching 1000 if you include the pre-merger black holes rather than the post-merger ones.
A scatter plot showing masses of detected black holes and neutron stars by LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA and electromagnetic observations, illustrating how gravitational wave astronomy is becoming a mature science. Solar masses are plotted on the y-axis by type.

Unlike the more common “Eagle plot” of gravitational wave events, this format showcases instead the population of post-merger black holes, sorted by mass, as detected in gravitational waves. On the left, in orange, neutron stars are indicated, while as we move to the right, blue colors appear, indicating black holes. The least massive post-merger remnant discovered in this fashion is under 3 solar masses, while the most massive one seen so far exceeds 200 solar masses.
Credit: LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA / Aaron Geller / Northwestern

Initially, physicists expected there would be a “mass gap” between about 3 and 5 solar masses, perhaps extending all the way up to 10 solar masses. It remains true that we don’t have a solid astrophysics theory for how to make black holes of that mass range directly from stars, but they clearly exist: we’ve seen them merging in two ways, in addition to the few black holes in that range detected electromagnetically.

    They arise from the merger of smaller-mass objects, like neutron stars.
    And they also arise as precursors to merging objects, where they merge with other objects to make an even heavier black hole.

From the graph above, sorted by the mass of the post-merger object, we can begin making population estimates for stellar mass black holes: how many of them form (from the pre-merger population) of a given mass as a function of the volume of the Universe over which our gravitational wave detectors are sensitive to that mass range. As you can see, there’s a large abundance of black holes between 5 and 20 solar masses, but still a significant abundance above that: going all the way up to the detection limit capabilities of LIGO, Virgo, and KAGRA.

You can also see, from the points along the curve where one of the pre-merger objects dips down to very low masses relative to the post-merger mass, that we have detected events with significantly mismatched masses, enabling us to test the predictions of Einstein’s theory for binary systems with very skewed mass ratios. (The theory passed, if you were wondering.)
Scatter plot titled "Masses in the Stellar Graveyard" showing solar masses of black holes (blue) and neutron stars (orange) detected by LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA, highlighting how gravitational wave astronomy has become a mature science.

This five-panel animation shows the evolution of black holes and neutron stars detected from gravitational wave events. The first, sparsest panel shows only the results from O1 and O2, the second and third panels add in the results from the first and second half of the O3 run (O3A and O3B), while the fourth and fifth panels add in the most recent results from O4A and O4B, with O4C still to come. All told, the number of confirmed detection events has now risen to 390, as of June 2026.
Credit: LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA / Aaron Geller / Northwestern

All in all, the newest data release, from the O4B run, adds in 161 new objects, while an additional 11 events — with the advent of reanalysis — have been mined from the earlier O4A run. The improvements that have resulted from the early days of LIGO to the modern era of LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA are even more impressive than you might expect from simply looking at the number of events and the populations of black holes and neutron stars that have been discovered. For example:

    The fourth observing run, with O4C still to come, already accounts for a full 77% of all gravitational wave events ever detected.
    The data reveals expectations for the abundance of “second generation” black holes: black holes that have arisen from the merger of objects that had already experienced mergers previously.
    The data gives us estimates for both the number of black holes and the total mass of black holes expected across the observable Universe, and it’s tremendous: around 40 quintillion black holes, representing 0.04% of the cosmic energy budget.
    The best sky localization ever has been obtained using gravitational waves alone: for event GW240615, which is localized to within just 6 square degrees: as opposed to thousands of square degrees for earlier events. (There are ~40,000 square degrees comprising the entire sky.)
    They also found the clearest, strongest, most significant gravitational wave signal ever in GW250114, enabling the most accurate test of general relativity ever, and confirming Stephen Hawking’s black hole area theorem.

Grid of colored time-series graphs titled "Gravitational-Wave Transient Catalog 5," showcasing a decade of binary black hole or neutron star merger detections as gravitational wave astronomy evolves into a mature science.

These time-frequency spectrographs show how the signal evolves over time (x-axis) and frequency (y-axis) for each merger event thus far. The brightest spots represent the strongest signal strengths, while the “uptick” at the end of each merger corresponds to the chirp that gets made during the final orbits prior to the merger in question. Note how significantly the event rate grew from O2 (orange) to O3 (blue), and from O3 to O4 (in red and violet), showcasing a remarkable increase in scientific power as driven by the upgrades made to the apparatuses and the addition of new detectors.
Credit: R. Nowicki/B. Smith/K. Jani (LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA/Vanderbilt)

Part of what makes this network of detectors so powerful is their complementary natures. Virgo and KAGRA are located in different positions and are oriented at different angles to the LIGO detectors in 3D space, meaning that when a gravitational wave signal passes through them:

    the incoming wave arrives at slightly different times in each location,
    and the gravitational wave alters the relative lengths of the arms in an angle-dependent fashion to the trajectory of the signal,

enabling gravitational wave astronomers to both test the predictions of Einstein’s relativity while simultaneously extracting more information about the location and nature of the event that generated the signal. The more detectors you have, the better you can do.

That’s why it’s such a big deal that in April of 2026, construction began on a fifth (relevant) gravitational wave detector here on Earth: LIGO India. Situated in a location with extremely low seismic noise and housing laser arms of identical 4-kilometer lengths to the LIGO detectors in Hanford and Livingston, it’s currently projected to begin science operations in 2030, joining the other four detectors for the anticipated fifth data run (O5) scheduled to take place between 2028 and 2031. With five detectors, three of which will have 4-kilometer arms (as opposed to Virgo’s and KAGRA’s 3-km arms), vastly superior localization of gravitational wave sources will finally be available: potentially reaching the single-square-degree level of precision for sufficiently strong signals.
An aerial view of a large campus with multiple buildings, two ponds, landscaped green areas, parking lots, and roads—a setting befitting the advancements of gravitational wave astronomy as it becomes a mature science.

This artist concept shows the layout of the LIGO India detector facility, showcasing the flat terrain, laser arms, and science complex at the center. All told, this 174-acre complex expects to begin science operations sometime in 2030, joining the other four active detectors.
Credit: LIGO India/LIGO Collaboration

What we’ve been able to accomplish, as a civilization, with the birth and growth of gravitational wave astronomy is a stunning example of how spectacularly we can advance when we invest sufficiently in high-risk, high-reward science. Without LIGO, Virgo and KAGRA never would have existed, and LIGO would not have come to fruition without major and sustained investments from the USA’s National Science Foundation. Yet, today, the USA is proposing slashing NSF funding by more than 50% while removing peer-review and science from the decision-making process of what gets funded.

Based on the merits of the underlying science — what’s already known and been established and what we can accomplish in the future — it’s an easy decision to not only continue advancing and upgrading the existing facilities, but to build a more advanced version of LIGO like Cosmic Explorer and funding a space-based gravitational wave detector like LISA. Contrary to the common narrative of competition breeding innovation, science works best when teams share knowledge and countries pool their resources to accomplish bigger things than any of them could do in isolation. The future of gravitational wave astronomy, barely imaginable a decade ago, is brighter than ever. It’s up to all of us not to squander the golden opportunity for discovery that nature has laid out before us.

For your ToE, this is **highly significant** because it shows that gravitational-wave astronomy has moved from “new detection method” to **mature precision science**.

In plain words:

> **Einstein gravity is not weakly supported. It is now tested by hundreds of black-hole and neutron-star merger events.**

The official GWTC-5 catalog says the cumulative gravitational-wave transient catalog now contains **390 candidates** with astrophysical probability (p_\mathrm{astro}\ge 0.5), adding O4b data from April 10, 2024 to January 28, 2025. It also says GW250114 exceeded network signal-to-noise ratio 70, allowing high-fidelity studies and tests of general relativity. ([arXiv][1])

```text
GR is now a precision-observation pillar,
not merely a theoretical pillar.
```

That raises the bar for your full GR target.

# 1. Elementary explanation

Before 2015, scientists had indirect evidence for gravitational waves.

Since 2015, they have directly measured them.

Now, by 2026, they have hundreds of detections.

That means scientists can now study black holes the way astronomers study stars:

```text
not one rare signal
but a population of many events
```

This is why the article says gravitational-wave astronomy is mature.

It is no longer only:

```text
Did we detect gravitational waves?
```

It is now:

```text
What kinds of black holes exist?
How often do they merge?
Do they obey general relativity?
Do they form in different environments?
Can we detect deviations from Einstein?
```

That matters enormously for your project.

---

# 2. Main ToE significance

The central significance is:

> **Gravity now has a large and growing observational testbed in the strong-field regime.**

Your ToE has a GR pillar. If the project wants “full mathematical completion,” the GR pillar cannot stop at:

This article reinforces that.

# 3. The strongest benchmark: GW250114

GW250114 is especially important.

The LVK paper on GW250114 says the event had network matched-filter signal-to-noise ratio 80, came from two near-equal-mass black holes, and allowed tests of Hawking’s area law and the Kerr nature of the remnant black hole. The analysis found that the remnant area is larger than the sum of the initial black-hole areas to high credibility. ([arXiv][2])

A second LVK paper says GW250114 is the loudest gravitational wave detected to date and gives one of the strongest single-event tests of general relativity and the Kerr nature of black holes. ([arXiv][3])

For your ToE, this means:

> **Any alternative or deeper gravity theory must reproduce events like GW250114 before it can be considered viable.**

That is a brutal but useful benchmark.

phys.org
Taking dark energy out of the equation: Mathematicians challenge the standard cosmological model of the universe
Who's behind this story?
8 - 10 minutes
universe

Credit: Unsplash/CC0 Public Domain

Mathematicians are challenging the idea that dark energy is responsible for the accelerating expansion of the universe. In a new paper published in Proceedings of the Royal Society A, mathematicians from the University of California, Davis, provide mathematical proof that instabilities inherent in the Einstein-Euler equations imply that the current model of the expanding universe is not viable.

The Einstein-Euler equations are a union of general relativity and fluid dynamics equations used to model astronomical phenomena such as galaxies, black holes, and cosmic expansion.

The research directly challenges the Lambda-cold dark matter model, the standard cosmological model of the Big Bang.

Study corresponding author Blake Temple, a distinguished professor emeritus of mathematics at UC Davis, compared the standard cosmological model to a pencil standing on its tip.

"All the forces are in balance when a pencil is standing on end, so it is a 'solution of the equations,'" he said. "But it's unstable. Any breath of air and it falls away."

The mathematics, Temple said, prove that Friedmann spacetimes—mathematical models that govern cosmic expansion—are unstable at both small and large length scales at the Big Bang, making it the most unstable solution of all.

"Unstable solutions in physics and science are considered not physical," Temple said. "You'll never observe them in nature."

Temple noted that this instability suggests a simpler explanation—one based entirely within the framework of Einstein's original theory.

"The instability of all Friedmann spacetimes to accelerated expansion suggests a simpler, more natural explanation for the acceleration of the universe than dark energy," he said.
Explaining the universe's accelerating expansion

Almost 30 years ago, dark energy was proposed as the force responsible for the accelerating expansion of the universe.

The idea harkens back to Albert Einstein's original 1915, gravity-describing equations for general relativity. To produce a static universe, Einstein initially introduced an antigravity factor in his theory. He called this factor the "cosmological constant."

After Edwin Hubble discovered the universe was expanding in 1929, Einstein famously called the cosmological constant his "biggest blunder" because without it he could have predicted the expansion.

However, the cosmological constant, and the idea that it's interchangeable with dark energy, was reintroduced to explain the universe's accelerating expansion in the 1990s. Standard cosmological models are based on what's called the "Friedmann universe," which describes all matter as expanding but being evenly distributed throughout space at each fixed time.

But the math didn't add up to Temple and his colleagues, leading them to pursue alternative explanations for the accelerating expansion of the universe.

"Our first idea was that maybe the universe was expanding because there was a shockwave, and the anomalous acceleration was the expanding wave behind that shockwave," Temple said. "Then we realized there's a family of self-similar solutions during the radiation epoch of the Big Bang, which might model that expanding wave."

Self-similar equations describe physical phenomena that maintain a pattern or structure, regardless of its scale.

In the current paper, the mathematicians use a self-similar version of the Einstein equations, which they derived in prior work, to represent the standard model of cosmology as a rest point of the equations. This provides the framework for a complete mathematical characterization of the standard model's stability, and more generally, the stability of all Friedmann spacetimes during the matter-dominated epoch of the Big Bang.

"We prove that, like Einstein's static model, the Friedmann spacetimes are all unstable to radial perturbation at large length scales," Temple said. "This appears to rule out the Lambda-cold dark matter model as a viable stable solution of the Einstein equations of general relativity, with or without dark energy."

"This means," he added, "that the Big Bang should generically look exactly like a Friedmann spacetime near the center of symmetry, but generically one should observe accelerations away from Friedmann far from the center."
Taking dark energy out of the equation: Mathematicians challenge the standard cosmological model of the universe

The phase portrait for the STV-ODE of order n = 2⁠. Credit: Proceedings of the Royal Society A Mathematical Physical and Engineering Science (2026). DOI: 10.1098/rspa.2025.0912
Rethinking the Copernican principle?

Temple and his colleagues found that the accelerating expansion of the universe is a direct consequence of the Einstein-Euler equations without the insertion of a cosmological constant or dark energy.

The math also calls into question the Copernican principle—the idea that Earth's location does not occupy a special place in the universe.

"Both the Lambda-cold dark matter model and a spherically symmetric spacetime produce a special place where we must lie for the model to be physically plausible," Temple said. "If this principle rules out one, it has to rule out the other."

## Direct answer

For your ToE, this is **highly significant for the `COSMO` pillar and the `GR-COSMO` seam**, but it should be treated as a **restricted mathematical challenge to ΛCDM**, not as a settled replacement for dark energy.

The main implication is:

> **Cosmic acceleration might not have to be inserted as a dark-energy term. It might, in some models, emerge from instability or structure inside Einstein’s equations themselves.**

That is very relevant to your project because your ToE keeps asking:

```text
Is this term fundamental?
Or is it an effective result of deeper structure?
```

This paper argues that Friedmann spacetimes can be unstable under radial perturbations in the Einstein-Euler equations, and that certain underdense perturbations can generate acceleration away from Friedmann behavior without a cosmological constant or dark energy. The authors frame this as a possible mechanism for accelerated expansion inside Einstein’s original equations. ([arXiv][1])

So the clean ToE takeaway is:

```text
dark energy as fundamental ingredient
vs.
dark-energy-like acceleration as emergent regime behavior
```

That is exactly the kind of distinction your full target map should track.

---

# 1. Elementary explanation

The standard cosmology model says the universe expands faster and faster because of something called **dark energy**.

In the usual model:

```text
dark energy pushes expansion
```

This new paper asks:

```text
What if expansion accelerates because the standard smooth-universe solution is unstable?
```

Imagine balancing a pencil on its tip.

Technically, the pencil standing straight up is a solution.

But it is unstable.

Tiny disturbances make it fall.

The authors are saying something similar about the usual smooth Friedmann universe:

```text
Friedmann universe = mathematical solution
but maybe unstable under certain perturbations
```

If that is right, cosmic acceleration might come from the universe naturally moving away from an unstable idealized state.

---

# 2. Why this matters for your ToE

Your ToE is trying to distinguish:

```text
fundamental law
```

from:

```text
effective behavior
```

This paper is a perfect example.

Dark energy may be:

```text
Λ is a real fundamental term in the equations
```

## Option B — effective

For your ToE, that means the `COSMO` pillar should not only ask:

---

# 3. What the paper actually claims

The paper studies pressureless Friedmann spacetimes using the Einstein-Euler equations in self-similar variables. It treats the critical (k=0) Friedmann spacetime as a stationary solution and characterizes it as an unstable saddle rest point. It also argues that underdense radial perturbations can evolve into solutions that accelerate away from Friedmann spacetimes at intermediate times, then decay back to the same leading-order Friedmann behavior asymptotically. ([arXiv][1])

In simpler language:

```text
standard smooth universe solution
→ perturb it radially
→ acceleration can appear
→ no Λ term required in that model
```

That is a serious mathematical claim.

But it is not the same as proving the real universe has no dark energy.

---

# 4. Why this is not a full replacement for ΛCDM yet

The standard ΛCDM model is not popular because physicists love mystery terms. It is popular because it fits a huge amount of data.

Planck’s 2018 cosmological-parameter paper found good consistency with the standard spatially flat six-parameter ΛCDM model using CMB temperature, polarization, and lensing data, and inferred values such as (H_0 = 67.4 \pm 0.5) km/s/Mpc and (\Omega_m = 0.315 \pm 0.007). ([arXiv][2])

So a dark-energy alternative must explain at least:

```text
CMB acoustic peaks
BAO distances
Type Ia supernovae
structure growth
lensing
Hubble tension behavior
large-scale isotropy
galaxy clustering
```

This new paper is a mathematical challenge to the stability of Friedmann models. It is not yet a full observational cosmology replacement.

Correct classification:

```text
important theoretical/cosmological benchmark
not established replacement for ΛCDM
```

What are the implications and significance of this for the ToE?


www.sciencedaily.com
Scientists found a surprisingly simple way to create powerful quantum states
6 - 7 minutes

Many of the most promising quantum technologies, including advanced sensors and future quantum computers, depend on a phenomenon known as entanglement, where particles become deeply connected and influence one another in ways that cannot be explained by classical physics. Creating the complex entangled states needed for these technologies has traditionally required sophisticated equipment and carefully designed experimental systems.

Researchers at the University of Chicago Pritzker School of Molecular Engineering (UChicago PME) have now proposed a much simpler approach. Their new theoretical method can generate and control a wide range of entangled quantum states using tools that are already common in many quantum physics laboratories.

The work, published in Physical Review X, could help advance ultra precise quantum sensing and open new opportunities for exploring fundamental physics.

"We wanted to take simple ingredients that you find in a lot of physical platforms and put these together in a minimal way to get something interesting, complex and powerful," said Aashish Clerk, professor of molecular engineering at UChicago PME and senior author of the new study.

The research was supported by Q-NEXT, a U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) National Quantum Information Science Research Center led by DOE's Argonne National Laboratory.

Rethinking Cavity QED Systems

The team's approach is based on cavity quantum electrodynamics, commonly known as cavity QED. In these experiments, atoms or other particles are placed inside an optical cavity, which consists of two mirrors that trap light between them. The particles then interact with the confined light inside the cavity.

A limitation of many cavity QED systems is that all of the atoms interact with the light in exactly the same way. Because the atoms are effectively indistinguishable, the range of quantum states that can be produced is restricted.

"The challenge has always been that these systems have too much symmetry. All the atoms are talking to light in the same way," Clerk said. "That really restricts what kind of entangled states you get."

In a typical cavity QED setup, each atom has a ground state and an excited state separated by a specific energy difference.

The researchers found a straightforward way to reduce the system's symmetry. While all atoms continue to be driven by the same laser, additional lasers or magnetic fields are used to shift the excited state energies of different groups of atoms. The atoms are arranged so that each one is paired with another atom that has an equal but opposite energy offset.

This simple modification allows atoms to behave differently from one another while preserving enough structure for the system to remain controllable and predictable. By changing which atoms receive particular energy shifts, scientists can tune the system to produce a variety of entangled states without altering the physical hardware.

"You turn these lasers on and wait, and at some point the system stabilizes into an interesting, highly entangled quantum state," said Anjun Chu, a postdoctoral researcher in the Clerk group and first author of the new work. "By simply adjusting the lasers, we can access kinds of entangled states that no one had thought about before."

Building Better Quantum Sensors

One of the most promising uses for the new approach is quantum sensing.

In theory, entangled quantum states can detect extremely small differences in magnetic fields or gravitational fields between separate locations. However, developing states that are both highly sensitive and resistant to noise has remained a major challenge.

The researchers demonstrated that a version of their proposed system containing two groups of atoms could be used to measure field gradients. When the two atomic ensembles are placed in different locations, the resulting quantum state reflects the difference between the local magnetic or gravitational fields. At the same time, it naturally rejects background noise that affects both locations equally.

"You're able to do two things that are normally not compatible with one another: Use entanglement to build an exquisitely sensitive sensor but also have robustness to arbitrarily large amounts of noise," Clerk said. "Normally, entanglement is very fragile. This approach has some amazing resilience."

Another advantage is that the information stored in these quantum states can be extracted using standard Ramsey measurement techniques, eliminating the need for specialized or exotic measurement methods.

Applications Beyond Sensing

The researchers also showed that the same platform can generate unusual quantum states that have long attracted interest from physicists.

One example is the AKLT state, a well known many body entangled state first introduced in the 1980s to describe unusual magnetic materials. The team found that their relatively simple setup can stabilize this state. In addition to helping scientists study complex magnetic systems, the AKLT state may also have applications in quantum computing.

Next Steps for the Research

The work remains theoretical for now, but the researchers are already discussing possible experimental tests with other groups.

They are also investigating more sophisticated ways to arrange atoms within the system and exploring the full range of quantum states that their method may be capable of producing.

"The fact that such simple ingredients can generate such complex and useful quantum states gives us hope that even before we reach the dream of a general all-purpose quantum computer, we can already generate quantum states that let us do things we couldn't do in a purely classical world," Clerk said.

This material is based upon work supported by the U.S. Department of Energy Office of Science National Quantum Information Science Research Centers as part of the Q-NEXT center.


## Direct answer

For your ToE, this is **important as a quantum-structure benchmark**, not as direct evidence for the theory.

Its significance is:

> **Simple ingredients, arranged with the right symmetry-breaking and dissipation rules, can generate highly complex entangled states.**

That is very aligned with your ToE’s core intuition:

```text
simple lawful structure
→ controlled constraints
→ complex physical behavior
```

The primary paper is *Reconfigurable dissipative entanglement between many spin ensembles: from robust quantum sensing to many-body state engineering*. It shows that a relatively simple cavity-QED setup can stabilize a broad family of pure, highly entangled states using collective decay plus local Hamiltonian terms, with applications to Heisenberg-limited differential sensing, common-mode noise immunity, and AKLT-state engineering. ([arXiv][1])

---

# 1. Elementary explanation

Normally, creating complicated entanglement is hard.

This study says:

> You may not need a huge complicated machine. You can take atoms in a light cavity, shift different groups of atoms slightly, and let the system settle into a powerful entangled state.

In simple words:

```text
same atoms + same cavity + carefully chosen offsets
→ useful entangled state
```

The important trick is that the researchers reduce too much symmetry. If all atoms behave exactly the same, the system is too limited. By giving different atom groups equal-and-opposite energy shifts, the system can create richer entangled states while still staying controllable.

---

# 2. Main ToE significance

The biggest ToE lesson is:

> **Complex order can come from simple rules plus the right constraints.**

That is directly relevant to your project.

Your ToE is not trying to say:

```text
everything is complex because we add many complicated parts
```

It is closer to:

```text
a small set of deep rules may generate many complex regimes
```

This paper is a real quantum-engineering example of that pattern.

It does not prove the ToE, but it supports the general research attitude:

> **Look for simple structural rules that produce rich physical behavior.**

---

# 3. Why symmetry matters

The article says typical cavity-QED systems have “too much symmetry” because all atoms interact with light in the same way.

That is a very ToE-relevant idea.

Sometimes symmetry is powerful because it gives conservation laws and clean structure.

But too much symmetry can make a system too rigid.

So the lesson is:

```text
symmetry gives order
controlled symmetry-breaking gives richness
```

For your ToE, that may matter for pillars and seams.

A seam may fail not because the framework is wrong, but because the bridge is too symmetric, too rigid, or missing a controlled asymmetry term.

That could matter especially for:

```text
QM-STAT
QFT-GR
EM-QFT
operational position
```

---

# 4. Dissipation as a constructive tool

This result uses **engineered dissipation**.

Usually, dissipation sounds bad. It means energy leaks away, coherence is lost, or the system decays.

But in this paper, dissipation is used constructively:

```text
system loses energy/information in a controlled way
→ system stabilizes into desired entangled state
```

That is important for your `STAT` pillar.

It suggests:

> **Loss, noise, decay, and entropy are not always merely destructive. Under the right structure, they can help build stable order.**

That is a very deep ToE lesson.

It connects to your broader idea that entropy/statistics may not be secondary bookkeeping. They may participate in how physical structure stabilizes.
